The new role demanded sustained attention to detailed performance metrics, careful sequencing of follow-ups, and long strategy sessions—environments where his tendency to mentally drift into creative ideas or guitar riffs in his head began to clash with expectations for focus and consistency. Over the following year, he found that despite his strong interpersonal skills and innovative training concepts, he was repeatedly missing key details in contracts, overlooking action items from meetings, and struggling to keep his mind from wandering during crucial calls, which contributed to friction with upper management and added strain during an already difficult marital separation. After his supervisor commented that his \"head seems somewhere else half the time\" and suggested coaching, Daniel, recognizing a lifelong pattern that was now clearly jeopardizing his career plans, requested a referral to a psychiatrist to find out why he was having so much trouble staying mentally present when it mattered most.\n- Chief Complaint: My mind keeps drifting off in meetings and calls, and I get distracted so easily that it’s starting to hurt my performance as a supervisor.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): With these bigger accounts, I’ll spend an hour on a sales report and still send it up with the wrong dates or a zero in the wrong place on the spreadsheet, because I skim instead of really reading what’s in front of me and miss small but important details in the contracts or numbers. \n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I start out after a meeting all fired up with a list of follow-ups, but by the time I’ve answered a couple of emails and taken a call, half those action items just never get done, or I do the first steps and then forget to actually close the loop with the client or my reps. \n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m constantly putting my work phone, badge, or notebook down in random spots—hotel rooms, the car, the office kitchen—and then wasting ten or fifteen minutes tearing the place apart because I can’t remember where I set them down two minutes earlier. \n- S008 (Distractibility): In these long strategy meetings or on conference calls, I’ll realize I’ve completely zoned out and I’m mentally working out a new guitar riff or thinking about my custody schedule instead of listening, and then I have to quietly ask someone later what I agreed to. \n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Lately I’m forgetting everyday stuff like paying a bill until I get a late notice, or realizing I never called a client or my lawyer back even though I remember clearly thinking, ‘I’ll do that as soon as this call ends,’ and then it just vanishes from my head. \n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): When I’m stuck in a long meeting or on a plane, it feels like my body is buzzing and I’m crawling out of my skin, and I keep shifting in my seat or bouncing my leg because sitting still for that long makes me really uncomfortable. \n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): Even when I try to relax at home, like watching a movie with my son or just listening to music, I end up talking over it, fiddling with my guitar, or checking my phone because I can’t just sit there quietly and do one calm thing. \n- S014 (Constant_Activity): My coworkers joke that I never stop moving because I’m always pacing the hallway, popping into people’s offices, or jumping straight from one task to another without really taking a break, even on days when I tell myself I’m going to slow down. \n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings, I’ll cut people off and finish their sentences or answer a question before my boss is done asking it, and then realize I jumped in too early or missed what they were actually getting at. \n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): Whether it’s waiting to speak on a group call, standing in line at the airport, or sitting in the lobby for an appointment, I get really impatient, fidgety, and annoyed, and I have to fight the urge to just push ahead or blurt out, ‘Can we speed this up?’\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_age_12_years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always writing on my report cards that I daydreamed in class, forgot to turn in homework I’d actually done, and couldn’t stay in my seat without talking or fidgeting, so this scattered, restless thing has been with me since I was a kid.\n- functional_impairment: Since taking on the bigger territory, my spacing out, lost items, and half-finished follow-ups have led to my boss questioning whether I’m ready for more responsibility, I’ve gotten written up for missing key details, and I’m constantly staying late to fix mistakes instead of having time with my son or taking care of divorce paperwork.\n- duration: These problems have Over the following year, he found that despite his strong interpersonal skills and innovative training concepts, he was repeatedly missing key details in contracts, overlooking action items from meetings, and struggling to keep his mind from wandering during crucial calls, which contributed to friction with upper management and added strain during an already difficult marital separation. After his supervisor commented that his \"head seems somewhere else half the time\" and suggested coaching, Daniel, recognizing a lifelong pattern that was now clearly jeopardizing his career plans, requested a referral to a psychiatrist to find out why he was having so much trouble staying mentally present when it mattered most.\n- Chief Complaint: My mind keeps drifting off in meetings and calls, and I get distracted so easily that it’s starting to hurt my performance as a supervisor.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): With these bigger accounts, I’ll spend an hour on a sales report and still send it up with the wrong dates or a zero in the wrong place on the spreadsheet, because I skim instead of really reading what’s in front of me and miss small but important details in the contracts or numbers. \n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I start out after a meeting all fired up with a list of follow-ups, but by the time I’ve answered a couple of emails and taken a call, half those action items just never get done, or I do the first steps and then forget to actually close the loop with the client or my reps. \n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m constantly putting my work phone, badge, or notebook down in random spots—hotel rooms, the car, the office kitchen—and then wasting ten or fifteen minutes tearing the place apart because I can’t remember where I set them down two minutes earlier. \n- S008 (Distractibility): In these long strategy meetings or on conference calls, I’ll realize I’ve completely zoned out and I’m mentally working out a new guitar riff or thinking about my custody schedule instead of listening, and then I have to quietly ask someone later what I agreed to. \n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Lately I’m forgetting everyday stuff like paying a bill until I get a late notice, or realizing I never called a client or my lawyer back even though I remember clearly thinking, ‘I’ll do that as soon as this call ends,’ and then it just vanishes from my head. \n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): When I’m stuck in a long meeting or on a plane, it feels like my body is buzzing and I’m crawling out of my skin, and I keep shifting in my seat or bouncing my leg because sitting still for that long makes me really uncomfortable. \n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): Even when I try to relax at home, like watching a movie with my son or just listening to music, I end up talking over it, fiddling with my guitar, or checking my phone because I can’t just sit there quietly and do one calm thing. \n- S014 (Constant_Activity): My coworkers joke that I never stop moving because I’m always pacing the hallway, popping into people’s offices, or jumping straight from one task to another without really taking a break, even on days when I tell myself I’m going to slow down. \n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings, I’ll cut people off and finish their sentences or answer a question before my boss is done asking it, and then realize I jumped in too early or missed what they were actually getting at. \n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): Whether it’s waiting to speak on a group call, standing in line at the airport, or sitting in the lobby for an appointment, I get really impatient, fidgety, and annoyed, and I have to fight the urge to just push ahead or blurt out, ‘Can we speed this up?’\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- onset_prior_to_age_12_years: Even back in elementary school, teachers were always writing on my report cards that I daydreamed in class, forgot to turn in homework I’d actually done, and couldn’t stay in my seat without talking or fidgeting, so this scattered, restless thing has been with me since I was a kid.\n- functional_impairment: Since taking on the bigger territory, my spacing out, lost items, and half-finished follow-ups have led to my boss questioning whether I’m ready for more responsibility, I’ve calls, and I get distracted so easily that it’s starting to hurt my performance as a supervisor.\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S001 (Detail_Inattention_Errors): With these bigger accounts, I’ll spend an hour on a sales report and still send it up with the wrong dates or a zero in the wrong place on the spreadsheet, because I skim instead of really reading what’s in front of me and miss small but important details in the contracts or numbers. \n- S004 (Instruction_Follow through_Difficulty): I start out after a meeting all fired up with a list of follow-ups, but by the time I’ve answered a couple of emails and taken a call, half those action items just never get done, or I do the first steps and then forget to actually close the loop with the client or my reps. \n- S007 (Losing_Items): I’m constantly putting my work phone, badge, or notebook down in random spots—hotel rooms, the car, the office kitchen—and then wasting ten or fifteen minutes tearing the place apart because I can’t remember where I set them down two minutes earlier. \n- S008 (Distractibility): In these long strategy meetings or on conference calls, I’ll realize I’ve completely zoned out and I’m mentally working out a new guitar riff or thinking about my custody schedule instead of listening, and then I have to quietly ask someone later what I agreed to. \n- S009 (Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness): Lately I’m forgetting everyday stuff like paying a bill until I get a late notice, or realizing I never called a client or my lawyer back even though I remember clearly thinking, ‘I’ll do that as soon as this call ends,’ and then it just vanishes from my head. \n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): When I’m stuck in a long meeting or on a plane, it feels like my body is buzzing and I’m crawling out of my skin, and I keep shifting in my seat or bouncing my leg because sitting still for that long makes me really uncomfortable. \n- S013 (Quiet_Activity_Difficulty): Even when I try to relax at home, like watching a movie with my son or just listening to music, I end up talking over it, fiddling with my guitar, or checking my phone because I can’t just sit there quietly and do one calm thing. \n- S014 (Constant_Activity): My coworkers joke that I never stop moving because I’m always pacing the hallway, popping into people’s offices, or jumping straight from one task to another without really taking a break, even on days when I tell myself I’m going to slow down. \n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings, I’ll cut people off and finish their sentences or answer a question before my boss is done asking it, and then realize I jumped in too early or missed what they were actually getting at. \n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): Whether it’s waiting to speak on a group call, standing in line at the airport, or sitting in the lobby for an appointment, I get really impatient, fidgety, and annoyed, and I have to fight the urge to just push ahead or Even in elementary school, teachers always noted on my report cards that I daydreamed, forgot to turn in homework, and couldn't stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues started in childhood but have worsened recently; can you tell me if you experienced significant difficulties with these symptoms during adolescence and young adulthood, or were you generally able to manage them until your recent life changes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've always had these tendencies throughout high school and my early career, but I was generally able to find ways to get by and manage them. The new role demanded sustained attention to detailed performance metrics, careful sequencing of follow-ups, and long strategy sessions—environments where his tendency to mentally drift into creative ideas or guitar riffs in his head began to clash with expectations for focus and consistency. Over the following year, he found that despite his strong interpersonal skills and innovative training concepts, he was repeatedly missing key details in contracts, overlooking action items from meetings, and struggling to keep his mind from wandering during crucial calls, which contributed to friction with upper management and added strain during an already difficult marital separation. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, I'm Daniel. My mind keeps drifting off in meetings and calls, and I get distracted so easily that it’s starting to hurt my performance as a supervisor. I really need to figure out why I'm having so much trouble staying mentally present when it matters most.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've also been experiencing difficulties with organization, following through on instructions, or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I often make careless mistakes on sales reports, like putting wrong dates or numbers on spreadsheets because I skim instead of reading closely. I'll also start strong after meetings but fail to follow through on action items once I get distracted by emails, and lately, I'm even forgetting daily tasks like paying bills on time or calling my lawyer back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling depressed or lost interest in things like playing my guitar. I'm definitely stressed and overwhelmed with my new work role and my divorce, but my main issue is feeling restless and completely unable to focus, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you're 'on the go' even when you're not actually doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. When I'm stuck in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing and crawling out of my skin, so I'm constantly shifting and bouncing my leg. Things like reckless driving or big spending sprees don't really sound like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms of inattention, restlessness, and impulsivity start when you were a child, or did they develop more recently, perhaps around the time of your new work role or divorce?\n\nPatient: They've definitely gotten much worse and more disruptive since my promotion about 14 months ago and my divorce, but these issues actually date back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, teachers always noted on my report cards that I daydreamed, forgot to turn in homework, and couldn't stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues started in childhood but have worsened recently; can you tell me if you experienced significant difficulties with these symptoms during adolescence and young adulthood, or were you generally able to manage them until your recent life changes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've always had these tendencies throughout high school and my early career, but I was generally able to find ways to get by and manage them. It wasn't until the promotion about 14 months ago and my marital separation hit at the same time that everything piled up and completely overwhelmed my ability to keep up.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been present since childhood but worsened recently, can you tell me more about how you managed them in adolescence and early adulthood, and what specific strategies you used to cope with them back then?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't really remember having any specific coping strategies back then. I think I mostly just scraped by or relied on last-minute pressure to get things done at the very last second. Life was just a lot less complicated to juggle back then, so I managed to get through somehow.\n\nDoctor: You've described feeling restless and having trouble sitting still, but can you tell me if you've experienced symptoms of hyperactivity, such as talking excessively or feeling an internal sense of restlessness, even when you're not physically active?"

The new role demanded sustained attention to detailed performance metrics, careful sequencing of follow-ups, and long strategy sessions—environments where his tendency to mentally drift into creative ideas or guitar riffs in his head began to clash with expectations for focus and consistency. Over the following year, he found that despite his strong interpersonal skills and innovative training concepts, he was repeatedly missing key details in contracts, overlooking action items from meetings, and struggling to keep his mind from wandering during crucial calls, which contributed to friction with upper management and added strain during an already difficult marital separation. After his supervisor commented that his \"head seems somewhere else half the time\" and suggested coaching, Daniel, recognizing a lifelong pattern that was now clearly jeopardizing his career plans, requested a referral to a psychiatrist to find out why he was having so much trouble staying mentally present when it mattered most.\n- Chief Complaint: My mind keeps drifting off in meetings and clearly thinking, ‘I’ll do that as soon as this call ends,’ and then it just vanishes from my head. \n- S012 (Feeling_Restless): When I’m stuck in a long meeting or on a plane, it feels like my body quietly and do one calm thing. \n- S014 (Constant_Activity): My coworkers joke that I never stop moving because I’m always pacing the hallway, popping into people’s offices, or jumping straight from one task to another without really taking a break, even on days when I tell myself I’m going to slow down. \n- S016 (Blurting_Answers): In meetings, I’ll cut people off and finish their sentences or answer a question before my boss is done asking it, and then realize I jumped in too early or missed what they were actually getting at. \n- S017 (Waiting_Turn_Difficulty): Whether it’s waiting to speak on a group call, standing in line at the airport, or sitting in the lobby for an appointment, I get really impatient, fidgety, and annoyed, and I have to fight the urge to just push ahead or gotten written up for missing key details, and I’m constantly staying late to fix mistakes instead of having time with my son or taking care of divorce paperwork.\n- duration: These problems have     "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, I'm Daniel. My mind keeps drifting off in meetings and calls, and I get distracted so easily that it’s starting to hurt my performance as a supervisor. I really need to figure out why I'm having so much trouble staying mentally present when it matters most.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've also been experiencing difficulties with organization, following through on instructions, or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I often make careless mistakes on sales reports, like putting wrong dates or numbers on spreadsheets because I skim instead of reading closely. I'll also start strong after meetings but fail to follow through on action items once I get distracted by emails, and lately, I'm even forgetting daily tasks like paying bills on time or calling my lawyer back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling depressed or lost interest in things like playing my guitar. I'm definitely stressed and overwhelmed with my new work role and my divorce, but my main issue is feeling restless and completely unable to focus, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you're 'on the go' even when you're not actually doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. When I'm stuck in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing and crawling out of my skin, so I'm constantly shifting and bouncing my leg. My coworkers even joke that I never stop moving because I'm always pacing the hallways, popping into offices, and jumping from one task to the next without taking a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others during conversations, blurted out answers before questions were finished, or had difficulty waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do that a lot. In meetings, I often cut people off, finish their sentences, or answer before they've even finished asking the question. I also get really impatient, fidgety, and annoyed whenever I have to wait my turn, whether it's waiting to speak on a group call or standing in lines at the airport.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself engaging in impulsive behaviors, such as spending large amounts of money impulsively, driving recklessly, or engaging in other risky behaviors without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really engage in those kinds of risky or impulsive behaviors. Things like reckless driving or big spending sprees don't really sound like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms of inattention, restlessness, and impulsivity start when you were a child, or did they develop more recently, perhaps around the time of your new work role or divorce?\n\nPatient: They've definitely gotten much worse and more disruptive since my promotion about 14 months ago and my divorce, but these issues actually date back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, teachers always noted on my report cards that I daydreamed, forgot to turn in homework, and couldn't stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues started in childhood but have worsened recently; can you tell me if you experienced significant difficulties with these symptoms during adolescence and young adulthood, or were you generally able to manage them until your recent life changes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've always had these tendencies throughout high school and my early career, but I was generally able to find ways to get by and manage them. It wasn't until the promotion about 14 months ago and my marital separation hit at the same time that everything piled up and completely overwhelmed my ability to keep up.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been present since childhood but worsened recently, can you tell me more about how you managed them in adolescence and early adulthood, and what specific strategies you used to cope with them back then?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't really remember having any specific coping strategies back then. I think I mostly just scraped by or relied on last-minute pressure to get things done at the very last second. Life was just a lot less complicated to juggle back then, so I managed to get through somehow.\n\nDoctor: You've described feeling restless and having trouble sitting still, but can you tell me if you've experienced symptoms of hyperactivity, such as talking excessively or feeling an internal sense of restlessness, even when you're not physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely experience both of those things. When I have to sit still in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing or I'm crawling out of my skin, making me bounce my leg constantly. Even at home trying to relax, I can't just sit quietly—I end up talking over movies, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started in childhood and have continued into adulthood, but can you tell me if you have experienced any periods of at least 6 months where you did not exhibit any of these symptoms, or have they been consistently present throughout your life?"
  }

No, I’ve never had a period of six months—or any time really—where these issues completely went away. It's been a consistent, lifelong pattern since I was a kid, though they’ve definitely become much more disruptive and noticeable since my promotion 14 months ago.

When I have to sit still in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing or I'm crawling out of my skin, making me bounce my leg constantly. Even at home trying to relax, I can't just sit quietly—I end up talking over movies, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started in childhood and have continued into adulthood, but can you tell me if you have experienced any periods of at least 6 months where you did not exhibit any of these symptoms, or have they been consistently present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had a period of six months—or any time really—where these issues completely went away. It's been a consistent, lifelong pattern since I was a kid, though they’ve definitely become much more disruptive and noticeable since my promotion 14 months ago."

I really need to figure out why I'm having so much trouble staying mentally present when it matters most.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've also been experiencing difficulties with organization, following through on instructions, or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I often make careless mistakes on sales reports, like putting wrong dates or numbers on spreadsheets because I skim instead of reading closely. I'll also start strong after meetings but fail to follow through on action items once I get distracted by emails, and lately, I'm even forgetting daily tasks like paying bills on time or calling my lawyer back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling depressed or lost interest in things like playing my guitar. I'm definitely stressed and overwhelmed with my new work role and my divorce, but my main issue is feeling restless and completely unable to focus, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you're 'on the go' even when you're not actually doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. When I'm stuck in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing and crawling out of my skin, so I'm constantly shifting and bouncing my leg. My coworkers even joke that I never stop moving because I'm always pacing the hallways, popping into offices, and jumping from one task to the next without taking a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others during conversations, blurted out answers before questions were finished, or had difficulty waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do that a lot. In meetings, I often cut people off, finish their sentences, or answer before they've even finished asking the question. I also get really impatient, fidgety, and annoyed whenever I have to wait my turn, whether it's waiting to speak on a group call or standing in lines at the airport.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself engaging in impulsive behaviors, such as spending large amounts of money impulsively, driving recklessly, or engaging in other risky behaviors without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really engage in those kinds of risky or impulsive behaviors. Things like reckless driving or big spending sprees don't really sound like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms of inattention, restlessness, and impulsivity start when you were a child, or did they develop more recently, perhaps around the time of your new work role or divorce?\n\nPatient: They've definitely gotten much worse and more disruptive since my promotion about 14 months ago and my divorce, but these issues actually date back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, teachers always noted on my report cards that I daydreamed, forgot to turn in homework, and couldn't stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues started in childhood but have worsened recently; can you tell me if you experienced significant difficulties with these symptoms during adolescence and young adulthood, or were you generally able to manage them until your recent life changes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've always had these tendencies throughout high school and my early career, but I was generally able to find ways to get by and manage them. It wasn't until the promotion about 14 months ago and my marital separation hit at the same time that everything piled up and completely overwhelmed my ability to keep up.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been present since childhood but worsened recently, can you tell me more about how you managed them in adolescence and early adulthood, and what specific strategies you used to cope with them back then?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't really remember having any specific coping strategies back then. I think I mostly just scraped by or relied on last-minute pressure to get things done at the very last second. Life was just a lot less complicated to juggle back then, so I managed to get through somehow.\n\nDoctor: You've described feeling restless and having trouble sitting still, but can you tell me if you've experienced symptoms of hyperactivity, such as talking excessively or feeling an internal sense of restlessness, even when you're not physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely experience both of those things. When I have to sit still in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing or I'm crawling out of my skin, making me bounce my leg constantly. Even at home trying to relax, I can't just sit quietly—I end up talking over movies, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started in childhood and have continued into adulthood, but can you tell me if you have experienced any periods of at least 6 months where you did not exhibit any of these symptoms, or have they been consistently present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had a period of six months—or any time really—where these issues completely went away. It's been a consistent, lifelong pattern since I was a kid, though they’ve definitely become much more disruptive and noticeable since my promotion 14 months ago.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your symptoms have affected your relationships, work performance, and daily life, and have you noticed any significant differences in how you function in different settings or environments?\n\nPatient: Since my promotion, these issues have severely impacted both my work and personal life. At work, my spacing out and errors led to a written write-up and my boss questioning my readiness for regional management, forcing me to stay late to fix mistakes instead of spending time with my son or handling my divorce paperwork. The high-demand, detail-heavy environment of this new role has made my lifelong patterns incredibly disruptive compared to my previous, less demanding routine."
  }

It's been a consistent, lifelong pattern since I was a kid, though they’ve definitely become much more disruptive and noticeable since my promotion 14 months ago.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your symptoms have affected your relationships, work performance, and daily life, and have you noticed any significant differences in how you function in different settings or environments?\n\nPatient: Since my promotion, these issues have severely impacted both my work and personal life. At work, my spacing out and errors led to a written write-up and my boss questioning my readiness for regional management, forcing me to stay late to fix mistakes instead of spending time with my son or handling my divorce paperwork. The high-demand, detail-heavy environment of this new role has made my lifelong patterns incredibly disruptive compared to my previous, less demanding routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your restlessness and hyperactivity symptoms manifest in different settings, such as at home, in social situations, or during leisure activities?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a big problem outside of work, especially when I'm trying to relax at home; if I try to watch a movie with my son, I can't just sit quietly and end up talking over it, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone. Also, when I'm forced to sit still for a while, like on a plane, my body literally feels like it's buzzing and I have to constantly shift around or bounce my leg just to cope."

I'm definitely stressed and overwhelmed with my new work role and my divorce, but my main issue is feeling restless and completely unable to focus, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you're 'on the go' even when you're not actually doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. When I'm stuck in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing and crawling out of my skin, so I'm constantly shifting and bouncing my leg. My coworkers even joke that I never stop moving because I'm always pacing the hallways, popping into offices, and jumping from one task to the next without taking a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others during conversations, blurted out answers before questions were finished, or had difficulty waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do that a lot. In meetings, I often cut people off, finish their sentences, or answer before they've even finished asking the question. Things like reckless driving or big spending sprees don't really sound like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms of inattention, restlessness, and impulsivity start when you were a child, or did they develop more recently, perhaps around the time of your new work role or divorce?\n\nPatient: They've definitely gotten much worse and more disruptive since my promotion about 14 months ago and my divorce, but these issues actually date back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, teachers always noted on my report cards that I daydreamed, forgot to turn in homework, and couldn't stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues started in childhood but have worsened recently; can you tell me if you experienced significant difficulties with these symptoms during adolescence and young adulthood, or were you generally able to manage them until your recent life changes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've always had these tendencies throughout high school and my early career, but I was generally able to find ways to get by and manage them. It wasn't until the promotion about 14 months ago and my marital separation hit at the same time that everything piled up and completely overwhelmed my ability to keep up.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been present since childhood but worsened recently, can you tell me more about how you managed them in adolescence and early adulthood, and what specific strategies you used to cope with them back then?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't really remember having any specific coping strategies back then. I think I mostly just scraped by or relied on last-minute pressure to get things done at the very last second. Life was just a lot less complicated to juggle back then, so I managed to get through somehow.\n\nDoctor: You've described feeling restless and having trouble sitting still, but can you tell me if you've experienced symptoms of hyperactivity, such as talking excessively or feeling an internal sense of restlessness, even when you're not physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely experience both of those things. When I have to sit still in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing or I'm crawling out of my skin, making me bounce my leg constantly. Even at home trying to relax, I can't just sit quietly—I end up talking over movies, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started in childhood and have continued into adulthood, but can you tell me if you have experienced any periods of at least 6 months where you did not exhibit any of these symptoms, or have they been consistently present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had a period of six months—or any time really—where these issues completely went away. It's been a consistent, lifelong pattern since I was a kid, though they’ve definitely become much more disruptive and noticeable since my promotion 14 months ago.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your symptoms have affected your relationships, work performance, and daily life, and have you noticed any significant differences in how you function in different settings or environments?\n\nPatient: Since my promotion, these issues have severely impacted both my work and personal life. At work, my spacing out and errors led to a written write-up and my boss questioning my readiness for regional management, forcing me to stay late to fix mistakes instead of spending time with my son or handling my divorce paperwork. The high-demand, detail-heavy environment of this new role has made my lifelong patterns incredibly disruptive compared to my previous, less demanding routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your restlessness and hyperactivity symptoms manifest in different settings, such as at home, in social situations, or during leisure activities?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a big problem outside of work, especially when I'm trying to relax at home; if I try to watch a movie with my son, I can't just sit quietly and end up talking over it, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone. Also, when I'm forced to sit still for a while, like on a plane, my body literally feels like it's buzzing and I have to constantly shift around or bounce my leg just to cope.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity have affected your daily life and relationships differently across various settings, such as work, home, and social situations, to help me understand the severity and impact of your symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s just compounding everything and making me feel incredibly overwhelmed. At work, my boss is questioning if I'm actually ready for this promotion and I've even been written up, which forces me to stay late to fix my mistakes. That leaves me with almost no quality time for my son, and I'm completely falling behind on my divorce and custody paperwork because my head is so scattered."

Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, I'm Daniel. My mind keeps drifting off in meetings and calls, and I get distracted so easily that it’s starting to hurt my performance as a supervisor. I really need to figure out why I'm having so much trouble staying mentally present when it matters most.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if you've also been experiencing difficulties with organization, following through on instructions, or making careless mistakes in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, absolutely. I often make careless mistakes on sales reports, like putting wrong dates or numbers on spreadsheets because I skim instead of reading closely. I'll also start strong after meetings but fail to follow through on action items once I get distracted by emails, and lately, I'm even forgetting daily tasks like paying bills on time or calling my lawyer back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad, empty, or depressed most of the day, nearly every day, or have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really been feeling depressed or lost interest in things like playing my guitar. I'm definitely stressed and overwhelmed with my new work role and my divorce, but my main issue is feeling restless and completely unable to focus, rather than feeling down.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting, tapping your hands or feet, or feeling like you're 'on the go' even when you're not actually doing anything physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. When I'm stuck in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing and crawling out of my skin, so I'm constantly shifting and bouncing my leg. My coworkers even joke that I never stop moving because I'm always pacing the hallways, popping into offices, and jumping from one task to the next without taking a break.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever interrupted others during conversations, blurted out answers before questions were finished, or had difficulty waiting your turn in conversations or lines?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do that a lot. In meetings, I often cut people off, finish their sentences, or answer before they've even finished asking the question. I also get really impatient, fidgety, and annoyed whenever I have to wait my turn, whether it's waiting to speak on a group call or standing in lines at the airport.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself engaging in impulsive behaviors, such as spending large amounts of money impulsively, driving recklessly, or engaging in other risky behaviors without thinking them through?\n\nPatient: No, I don't really engage in those kinds of risky or impulsive behaviors. Things like reckless driving or big spending sprees don't really sound like me at all.\n\nDoctor: Did these symptoms of inattention, restlessness, and impulsivity start when you were a child, or did they develop more recently, perhaps around the time of your new work role or divorce?\n\nPatient: They've definitely gotten much worse and more disruptive since my promotion about 14 months ago and my divorce, but these issues actually date back to my childhood. Even in elementary school, teachers always noted on my report cards that I daydreamed, forgot to turn in homework, and couldn't stay in my seat.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that these issues started in childhood but have worsened recently; can you tell me if you experienced significant difficulties with these symptoms during adolescence and young adulthood, or were you generally able to manage them until your recent life changes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I've always had these tendencies throughout high school and my early career, but I was generally able to find ways to get by and manage them. It wasn't until the promotion about 14 months ago and my marital separation hit at the same time that everything piled up and completely overwhelmed my ability to keep up.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that these symptoms have been present since childhood but worsened recently, can you tell me more about how you managed them in adolescence and early adulthood, and what specific strategies you used to cope with them back then?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I don't really remember having any specific coping strategies back then. I think I mostly just scraped by or relied on last-minute pressure to get things done at the very last second. Life was just a lot less complicated to juggle back then, so I managed to get through somehow.\n\nDoctor: You've described feeling restless and having trouble sitting still, but can you tell me if you've experienced symptoms of hyperactivity, such as talking excessively or feeling an internal sense of restlessness, even when you're not physically active?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely experience both of those things. When I have to sit still in meetings or on planes, my body feels like it's buzzing or I'm crawling out of my skin, making me bounce my leg constantly. Even at home trying to relax, I can't just sit quietly—I end up talking over movies, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned that your symptoms started in childhood and have continued into adulthood, but can you tell me if you have experienced any periods of at least 6 months where you did not exhibit any of these symptoms, or have they been consistently present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never had a period of six months—or any time really—where these issues completely went away. It's been a consistent, lifelong pattern since I was a kid, though they’ve definitely become much more disruptive and noticeable since my promotion 14 months ago.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your symptoms have affected your relationships, work performance, and daily life, and have you noticed any significant differences in how you function in different settings or environments?\n\nPatient: Since my promotion, these issues have severely impacted both my work and personal life. At work, my spacing out and errors led to a written write-up and my boss questioning my readiness for regional management, forcing me to stay late to fix mistakes instead of spending time with my son or handling my divorce paperwork. The high-demand, detail-heavy environment of this new role has made my lifelong patterns incredibly disruptive compared to my previous, less demanding routine.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about how your restlessness and hyperactivity symptoms manifest in different settings, such as at home, in social situations, or during leisure activities?\n\nPatient: It's definitely a big problem outside of work, especially when I'm trying to relax at home; if I try to watch a movie with my son, I can't just sit quietly and end up talking over it, fiddling with my guitar, or constantly checking my phone. Also, when I'm forced to sit still for a while, like on a plane, my body literally feels like it's buzzing and I have to constantly shift around or bounce my leg just to cope.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your symptoms of inattention, hyperactivity, and impulsivity have affected your daily life and relationships differently across various settings, such as work, home, and social situations, to help me understand the severity and impact of your symptoms?\n\nPatient: It’s just compounding everything and making me feel incredibly overwhelmed. At work, my boss is questioning if I'm actually ready for this promotion and I've even been written up, which forces me to stay late to fix my mistakes. That leaves me with almost no quality time for my son, and I'm completely falling behind on my divorce and custody paperwork because my head is so scattered.\n\nDoctor: Can you confirm that you have experienced significant symptoms of inattention and hyperactivity-impulsivity in multiple settings, including work, home, and social situations, consistently over time since childhood, without any significant periods of remission?"
  }
]

Yes, I can absolutely confirm that. Since elementary school, when teachers complained about my daydreaming, fidgeting, and forgotten homework, these struggles have been a constant presence in my life without any breaks. It affects me everywhere—from spacing out and getting written up at work, to forgetting bills and struggling to relax with my son at home.

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n[S013] Quiet_Activity_Difficulty: Often unable to play or engage in leisure activities quietly.\n[S014] Constant_Activity: Is often 'on the go', acting as if 'driven by a motor'.\n[S015] Excessive_Talking: Often talks excessively.\n[S016] Blurting_Answers: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n[S017] Waiting_Turn_Difficulty: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n[S018] Interrupting_Intruding: Often interrupts or intrudes on others (e.g., butts into conversations, games, or activities).\n[S019] Delusions: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence. Subtypes: Auditory Hallucinations, Visual Hallucinations, Tactile Hallucinations, Olfactory Hallucinations, Gustatory Hallucinations\n[S021] Disorganized_Thinking_Speech: Symptoms where speech or thought appears scattered and difficult to follow due to a lack of logical or consistent flow.  Subtypes: Derailment or Loose Associations, Tangentiality, Incoherence or Word Salad, Neologisms, Clang Associations\n[S022] Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior: A state where behavior and motor control are severely disturbed, appearing as movements or reactions inappropriate to the situation.  Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost. Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.
